Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I don’t feel like myself anymore. It started after they changed my hours at the warehouse and brought in this new inventory system, and I know that might not sound like enough to make a grown man fall apart, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nI’m 74, and I’ve worked hard my whole life, and I’ve always been someone people could count on. But lately I’ve been missing shifts, missing things I said I’d do at church, and then I feel ashamed about it. I get short with my family—snapping over little things that don’t deserve that kind of reaction. And some mornings I’m sitting there already dressed, boots on, just staring, wondering if I can even make myself go back into that place.\n\nIt’s like since those changes happened, everything in me has been wound too tight, and I can’t seem to get back on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things anymore—like activities you used to enjoy, such as hobbies, time with family, or going to church?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I still care about those things. I still want to see my family, and church still matters to me. It’s not like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing meant anything anymore.\n\nBut I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to whether I’m enjoying them the same way. When I’m there, my mind can be kind of occupied, thinking about everything else, and I might not settle into it like I used to. But I wouldn’t say I’ve completely lost pleasure in things, no. It’s more like I’m distracted or tense, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot more than usual, or feeling anxious about things, even when there isn’t a clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around anxious for no reason, exactly. There usually is a reason in my head, or at least something I can point to, even if maybe it’s bigger in my mind than it ought to be.\n\nLike, if I’m worrying, it’s tied to work, or whether I’m going to mess something up, or whether people are noticing I’m not handling things the way I used to. It’s not just coming out of nowhere while I’m sitting peacefully, you know? But I do feel more wound up than I used to, and once I start thinking about something, it can kind of keep going around and around.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. More worried than usual, probably. But not for no reason. At least it doesn’t feel like no reason to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about more than just work or specific things — like a general sense that you're worried about a lot of different things throughout the day, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it spreads out to everything, no. I mean, it can feel like it follows me around even when I’m not at the warehouse, because I’ll be sitting at home and still turning it over in my head. But when I try to trace it back, it usually has a thread tied to work, or to whether I’m keeping up with what I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike if I’m thinking about church or family, it’s not that I’m suddenly worried about a hundred separate things for no reason. It’s more that I’m worried I’m letting people down, or that I’m not acting like myself, and then that loops back into this whole mess of feeling behind and not steady. So… I don’t know. It feels broader than just the building I work in, but I wouldn’t say I’m worried about every little thing all day long. It’s more like one main worry casts a shadow over the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling on edge or tense most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem or situation that you're actively dealing with?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot, but whether it’s “most days” and whether there’s no specific reason… that’s where I get a little stuck.\n\nUsually, if I’m on edge, I can connect it to something. It might not be something happening right that second, but it’s in my mind somewhere. Like I’ll be sitting there and I feel my shoulders tight or I feel like I can’t quite relax, and if I think about it long enough, I can usually trace it back to some concern or something I’m bracing for.\n\nI don’t think I’m just tense out of the blue, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I carry things with me longer than I probably should, so even when the situation isn’t right in front of me, my body is still acting like it is. But I can’t honestly say I’ve been keeping track closely enough to know how often.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or like you don’t have much energy lately, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, at my age and with the kind of work I do, I expect to be tired sometimes, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal from what’s something different.\n\nIf I’ve been moving stock around or on my feet a lot, then sure, I get worn out, but that doesn’t feel mysterious to me. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. I mean, I sleep… maybe not always perfectly, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether I’m waking up more than usual or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there for a bit, but I think most people do that, especially when you’ve got things on your mind or you’re getting older. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. At 74, there are days where I’m not exactly springing out of a chair, you know? But that feels more like just normal stiffness or taking my time, not something where I’d say, “What’s wrong with me, why am I moving so slow?”\n\nAnd as far as being restless, unable to sit still… I don’t really think of myself that way. I might shift around or get up if I’m uncomfortable or if something’s on my mind, but I haven’t noticed pacing around or feeling like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit and think too much sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not something that stands out to me. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things throughout the day, even small or minor issues, not just the bigger stressors like work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a bunch of little things all day long, no. I mean, I worry, sure—everybody worries, and I probably get stuck thinking about certain things more than I’d like sometimes. But when you say “a variety of different things,” like I’m jumping from one small issue to another all day, I don’t know that I recognize that in myself.\n\nIf something’s important or it feels like it needs handling, then yes, it can stay in my head. I might go over it more than once, think about what I should’ve done or what I need to do next. But I don’t usually catch myself worrying about every minor thing, like, I don’t know, a tiny errand or some small inconvenience. At least it doesn’t stand out to me that way.\n\nI guess I have a hard time separating normal thinking from worrying, too. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t necessarily call it worry about lots of different things. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in that broad, constant kind of way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious most days, even when there isn’t something specific happening that would explain it, like a general background feeling of anxiety that’s always there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it as a constant background anxiety, no. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. I might be tense or preoccupied and not really call it anxiety, you know? But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. But I can’t honestly say I’ve been keeping track closely enough to know how often.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or like you don’t have much energy lately, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, at my age and with the kind of work I do, I expect to be tired sometimes, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal from what’s something different.\n\nIf I’ve been moving stock around or on my feet a lot, then sure, I get worn out, but that doesn’t feel mysterious to me. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. I mean, I sleep… maybe not always perfectly, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether I’m waking up more than usual or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there for a bit, but I think most people do that, especially when you’ve got things on your mind or you’re getting older. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. At 74, there are days where I’m not exactly springing out of a chair, you know? But that feels more like just normal stiffness or taking my time, not something where I’d say, “What’s wrong with me, why am I moving so slow?”\n\nAnd as far as being restless, unable to sit still… I don’t really think of myself that way. I might shift around or get up if I’m uncomfortable or if something’s on my mind, but I haven’t noticed pacing around or feeling like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit and think too much sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not something that stands out to me. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things throughout the day, even small or minor issues, not just the bigger stressors like work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a bunch of little things all day long, no. I mean, I worry, sure—everybody worries, and I probably get stuck thinking about certain things more than I’d like sometimes. But when you say “a variety of different things,” like I’m jumping from one small issue to another all day, I don’t know that I recognize that in myself.\n\nIf something’s important or it feels like it needs handling, then yes, it can stay in my head. I might go over it more than once, think about what I should’ve done or what I need to do next. But I don’t usually catch myself worrying about every minor thing, like, I don’t know, a tiny errand or some small inconvenience. At least it doesn’t stand out to me that way.\n\nI guess I have a hard time separating normal thinking from worrying, too. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t necessarily call it worry about lots of different things. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in that broad, constant kind of way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious most days, even when there isn’t something specific happening that would explain it, like a general background feeling of anxiety that’s always there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it as a constant background anxiety, no. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. I might be tense or preoccupied and not really call it anxiety, you know? But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I know it might sound like I’m splitting hairs, but when you say “many different things,” I picture someone worrying about one thing, then another, then another, all unrelated, like the car, the bills, the weather, the neighbor, some appointment, and it just keeps jumping around for no clear reason. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. Or if I am, I’m not recognizing it as that.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, but I have trouble saying whether that’s worry or just thinking too much. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are times when I’ll tell myself, “Alright, stop going over this, think about something else,” and my mind may still circle back to whatever it was. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. But if everything seems settled and there isn’t anything I can point to, I don’t really recognize myself as worrying anyway. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or low on energy even on days when you haven’t been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Some days I’m not exactly energetic, but I wouldn’t automatically say, “Oh, I’m tired even though I didn’t do anything.” It’s hard to separate out.\n\nIf I’ve been moving around or doing things, then being tired makes sense to me. On quieter days, I might feel a little slowed down, maybe not as ready to get going, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I’m wiped out for no reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, if everything really seems okay on the surface, I don’t usually catch myself thinking, “There’s a problem,” when I know there isn’t one. But sometimes my mind will still kind of poke around at things, if that makes sense.

I mean, at my age and with the kind of work I do, I expect to be tired sometimes, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal from what’s something different.\n\nIf I’ve been moving stock around or on my feet a lot, then sure, I get worn out, but that doesn’t feel mysterious to me. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. I mean, I sleep… maybe not always perfectly, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether I’m waking up more than usual or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there for a bit, but I think most people do that, especially when you’ve got things on your mind or you’re getting older. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. At 74, there are days where I’m not exactly springing out of a chair, you know? But that feels more like just normal stiffness or taking my time, not something where I’d say, “What’s wrong with me, why am I moving so slow?”\n\nAnd as far as being restless, unable to sit still… I don’t really think of myself that way. I might shift around or get up if I’m uncomfortable or if something’s on my mind, but I haven’t noticed pacing around or feeling like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit and think too much sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not something that stands out to me. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things throughout the day, even small or minor issues, not just the bigger stressors like work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a bunch of little things all day long, no. I mean, I worry, sure—everybody worries, and I probably get stuck thinking about certain things more than I’d like sometimes. But when you say “a variety of different things,” like I’m jumping from one small issue to another all day, I don’t know that I recognize that in myself.\n\nIf something’s important or it feels like it needs handling, then yes, it can stay in my head. I might go over it more than once, think about what I should’ve done or what I need to do next. But I don’t usually catch myself worrying about every minor thing, like, I don’t know, a tiny errand or some small inconvenience. At least it doesn’t stand out to me that way.\n\nI guess I have a hard time separating normal thinking from worrying, too. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t necessarily call it worry about lots of different things. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in that broad, constant kind of way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious most days, even when there isn’t something specific happening that would explain it, like a general background feeling of anxiety that’s always there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it as a constant background anxiety, no. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. I might be tense or preoccupied and not really call it anxiety, you know? But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I know it might sound like I’m splitting hairs, but when you say “many different things,” I picture someone worrying about one thing, then another, then another, all unrelated, like the car, the bills, the weather, the neighbor, some appointment, and it just keeps jumping around for no clear reason. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. Or if I am, I’m not recognizing it as that.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, but I have trouble saying whether that’s worry or just thinking too much. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are times when I’ll tell myself, “Alright, stop going over this, think about something else,” and my mind may still circle back to whatever it was. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. But if everything seems settled and there isn’t anything I can point to, I don’t really recognize myself as worrying anyway. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or low on energy even on days when you haven’t been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, at 74, I don’t always know what’s just being older and what’s something I’m supposed to pay closer attention to. Some days I’m not exactly energetic, but I wouldn’t automatically say, “Oh, I’m tired even though I didn’t do anything.” It’s hard to separate out.\n\nIf I’ve been moving around or doing things, then being tired makes sense to me. On quieter days, I might feel a little slowed down, maybe not as ready to get going, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I’m wiped out for no reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when you know, logically, that there isn’t really a problem, like when everything seems to be going okay on the surface?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, if everything really seems okay on the surface, I don’t usually catch myself thinking, “There’s a problem,” when I know there isn’t one. But sometimes my mind will still kind of poke around at things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I might think, “Well, maybe I’m missing something,” or I’ll go back over something that happened and wonder if I handled it right. But I’m not sure whether that counts as worrying when there’s no real problem, or whether it’s just me being in my own head too much.\n\nI guess logically, if things are fine, I can usually tell myself they’re fine. I don’t think I’m sitting there inventing trouble out of nowhere. But I can be a little slow to let something go if it feels unsettled, even if nothing is actually wrong at that moment. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly, because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exact thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I mean… “down” is one of those words where I’m not always sure what people mean by it. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around cheerful all the time, but I also don’t know that I’d say I’m depressed most days for no reason.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say it spreads across a lot of different areas like that. I mean, I can worry, sure, but I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about work, family, health, responsibilities, all separate things at once.

Sometimes one thing will get in my head and I’ll kind of chew on it longer than I probably need to, but it’s usually tied to whatever set it off. I don’t think I’ve noticed it jumping from one category to another, like, “Now I’m worried about this, now that, now something else.” At least not in a way I could clearly point to.

So I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say yes strongly. It doesn’t feel like many different areas all at the same time, or unrelated worries piling up, if that’s what you mean.

But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I know it might sound like I’m splitting hairs, but when you say “many different things,” I picture someone worrying about one thing, then another, then another, all unrelated, like the car, the bills, the weather, the neighbor, some appointment, and it just keeps jumping around for no clear reason. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. Or if I am, I’m not recognizing it as that.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, but I have trouble saying whether that’s worry or just thinking too much. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are times when I’ll tell myself, “Alright, stop going over this, think about something else,” and my mind may still circle back to whatever it was. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. But if everything seems settled and there isn’t anything I can point to, I don’t really recognize myself as worrying anyway. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or low on energy even on days when you haven’t been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, at 74, I don’t always know what’s just being older and what’s something I’m supposed to pay closer attention to. Some days I’m not exactly energetic, but I wouldn’t automatically say, “Oh, I’m tired even though I didn’t do anything.” It’s hard to separate out.\n\nIf I’ve been moving around or doing things, then being tired makes sense to me. On quieter days, I might feel a little slowed down, maybe not as ready to get going, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I’m wiped out for no reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when you know, logically, that there isn’t really a problem, like when everything seems to be going okay on the surface?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly, because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exact thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or depressed most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself feeling sad just out of nowhere when everything is fine.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not clearly. I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I mean, I’ve been thinking about that kind of thing because people ask, “Are you still doing the things you like?” and I don’t always know how to measure it.

There are times when I don’t feel as eager to start something, maybe I’ll put it off or I’ll think, “Eh, maybe later,” but I don’t know if that means I don’t care about it anymore. Sometimes once I’m actually there or doing the thing, it’s not like I hate it or feel nothing. It’s more like getting myself to it can feel harder, if that makes sense.

With family or normal routines, I still know they matter to me. I wouldn’t say I stopped caring. Looking forward to things… that’s a little harder. I don’t know that I look forward to things in the same clear way all the time, but I also can’t point to something and say, “I used to enjoy that and now I just don’t.” I haven’t really noticed it in a definite way.

So I guess my answer is maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more that I may be less enthusiastic sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.

It’s not just coming out of nowhere while I’m sitting peacefully, you know? I mean, I know I feel tense a lot, but whether it’s “most days” and whether there’s no specific reason… that’s where I get a little stuck.\n\nUsually, if I’m on edge, I can connect it to something. It might not be something happening right that second, but it’s in my mind somewhere. Like I’ll be sitting there and I feel my shoulders tight or I feel like I can’t quite relax, and if I think about it long enough, I can usually trace it back to some concern or something I’m bracing for.\n\nI don’t think I’m just tense out of the blue, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I carry things with me longer than I probably should, so even when the situation isn’t right in front of me, my body is still acting like it is. I mean, at my age and with the kind of work I do, I expect to be tired sometimes, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal from what’s something different.\n\nIf I’ve been moving stock around or on my feet a lot, then sure, I get worn out, but that doesn’t feel mysterious to me. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. I mean, I sleep… maybe not always perfectly, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether I’m waking up more than usual or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there for a bit, but I think most people do that, especially when you’ve got things on your mind or you’re getting older. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. At 74, there are days where I’m not exactly springing out of a chair, you know? But that feels more like just normal stiffness or taking my time, not something where I’d say, “What’s wrong with me, why am I moving so slow?”\n\nAnd as far as being restless, unable to sit still… I don’t really think of myself that way. I might shift around or get up if I’m uncomfortable or if something’s on my mind, but I haven’t noticed pacing around or feeling like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit and think too much sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not something that stands out to me. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things throughout the day, even small or minor issues, not just the bigger stressors like work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a bunch of little things all day long, no. I mean, I worry, sure—everybody worries, and I probably get stuck thinking about certain things more than I’d like sometimes. But when you say “a variety of different things,” like I’m jumping from one small issue to another all day, I don’t know that I recognize that in myself.\n\nIf something’s important or it feels like it needs handling, then yes, it can stay in my head. I might go over it more than once, think about what I should’ve done or what I need to do next. But I don’t usually catch myself worrying about every minor thing, like, I don’t know, a tiny errand or some small inconvenience. At least it doesn’t stand out to me that way.\n\nI guess I have a hard time separating normal thinking from worrying, too. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t necessarily call it worry about lots of different things. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in that broad, constant kind of way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious most days, even when there isn’t something specific happening that would explain it, like a general background feeling of anxiety that’s always there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it as a constant background anxiety, no. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. I might be tense or preoccupied and not really call it anxiety, you know? But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I know it might sound like I’m splitting hairs, but when you say “many different things,” I picture someone worrying about one thing, then another, then another, all unrelated, like the car, the bills, the weather, the neighbor, some appointment, and it just keeps jumping around for no clear reason. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. Or if I am, I’m not recognizing it as that.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, but I have trouble saying whether that’s worry or just thinking too much. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are times when I’ll tell myself, “Alright, stop going over this, think about something else,” and my mind may still circle back to whatever it was. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. But if everything seems settled and there isn’t anything I can point to, I don’t really recognize myself as worrying anyway. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or low on energy even on days when you haven’t been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, at 74, I don’t always know what’s just being older and what’s something I’m supposed to pay closer attention to. Some days I’m not exactly energetic, but I wouldn’t automatically say, “Oh, I’m tired even though I didn’t do anything.” It’s hard to separate out.\n\nIf I’ve been moving around or doing things, then being tired makes sense to me. On quieter days, I might feel a little slowed down, maybe not as ready to get going, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I’m wiped out for no reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when you know, logically, that there isn’t really a problem, like when everything seems to be going okay on the surface?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, if everything really seems okay on the surface, I don’t usually catch myself thinking, “There’s a problem,” when I know there isn’t one. But sometimes my mind will still kind of poke around at things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I might think, “Well, maybe I’m missing something,” or I’ll go back over something that happened and wonder if I handled it right. But I’m not sure whether that counts as worrying when there’s no real problem, or whether it’s just me being in my own head too much.\n\nI guess logically, if things are fine, I can usually tell myself they’re fine. I don’t think I’m sitting there inventing trouble out of nowhere. But I can be a little slow to let something go if it feels unsettled, even if nothing is actually wrong at that moment. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly, because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exact thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or depressed most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I mean… “down” is one of those words where I’m not always sure what people mean by it. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around cheerful all the time, but I also don’t know that I’d say I’m depressed most days for no reason.\n\nSome days I’m quieter, or I don’t feel like myself, and I may not have much enthusiasm, but I don’t always stop and label it as sadness. And if I do feel low, I usually feel like there’s something connected to it, even if it’s not some big dramatic thing. I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself feeling sad just out of nowhere when everything is fine.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not clearly. I may have days like that, but I don’t know that it’s most days, and I don’t know that I’d call it depressed exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different areas of your life—like work, family, health, or other responsibilities—even when those worries aren’t directly connected to each other?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it spreads across a lot of different areas like that. I mean, I can worry, sure, but I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about work, family, health, responsibilities, all separate things at once.\n\nSometimes one thing will get in my head and I’ll kind of chew on it longer than I probably need to, but it’s usually tied to whatever set it off. I don’t think I’ve noticed it jumping from one category to another, like, “Now I’m worried about this, now that, now something else.” At least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say yes strongly. It doesn’t feel like many different areas all at the same time, or unrelated worries piling up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you found that you don’t care about or look forward to things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or going to church, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I mean, I’ve been thinking about that kind of thing because people ask, “Are you still doing the things you like?” and I don’t always know how to measure it.\n\nThere are times when I don’t feel as eager to start something, maybe I’ll put it off or I’ll think, “Eh, maybe later,” but I don’t know if that means I don’t care about it anymore. Sometimes once I’m actually there or doing the thing, it’s not like I hate it or feel nothing. It’s more like getting myself to it can feel harder, if that makes sense.\n\nWith family or normal routines, I still know they matter to me. I wouldn’t say I stopped caring. Looking forward to things… that’s a little harder. I don’t know that I look forward to things in the same clear way all the time, but I also can’t point to something and say, “I used to enjoy that and now I just don’t.” I haven’t really noticed it in a definite way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can say I’ve had days where I truly didn’t care about anything. That sounds… stronger than what I’ve noticed in myself.

I mean, there are definitely days where I’m kind of flat, or where I don’t feel much spark about doing things. Like something I normally might enjoy, I might look at it and think, “I don’t know, not right now,” or it takes more effort to get into it. But once I’m with family or doing something familiar, I don’t think it’s like I can’t feel any pleasure at all. It’s not completely gone.

Church, family, little routines… I still know those things matter. I may not always feel excited beforehand, but I wouldn’t say I don’t care. And “couldn’t feel any pleasure” feels like a pretty absolute way to put it. I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not in a way I’d recognize and say, yes, that was a whole day where nothing meant anything.

So… no, not really. Maybe less enthusiasm sometimes, but not truly no caring or no pleasure.

It’s not just coming out of nowhere while I’m sitting peacefully, you know? At least it doesn’t feel like no reason to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about more than just work or specific things — like a general sense that you're worried about a lot of different things throughout the day, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it spreads out to everything, no. I mean, it can feel like it follows me around even when I’m not at the warehouse, because I’ll be sitting at home and still turning it over in my head. But when I try to trace it back, it usually has a thread tied to work, or to whether I’m keeping up with what I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike if I’m thinking about church or family, it’s not that I’m suddenly worried about a hundred separate things for no reason. It’s more that I’m worried I’m letting people down, or that I’m not acting like myself, and then that loops back into this whole mess of feeling behind and not steady. So… I don’t know. It feels broader than just the building I work in, but I wouldn’t say I’m worried about every little thing all day long. It’s more like one main worry casts a shadow over the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling on edge or tense most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem or situation that you're actively dealing with?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot, but whether it’s “most days” and whether there’s no specific reason… that’s where I get a little stuck.\n\nUsually, if I’m on edge, I can connect it to something. It might not be something happening right that second, but it’s in my mind somewhere. Like I’ll be sitting there and I feel my shoulders tight or I feel like I can’t quite relax, and if I think about it long enough, I can usually trace it back to some concern or something I’m bracing for.\n\nI don’t think I’m just tense out of the blue, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I carry things with me longer than I probably should, so even when the situation isn’t right in front of me, my body is still acting like it is. But I can’t honestly say I’ve been keeping track closely enough to know how often.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or like you don’t have much energy lately, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, at my age and with the kind of work I do, I expect to be tired sometimes, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal from what’s something different.\n\nIf I’ve been moving stock around or on my feet a lot, then sure, I get worn out, but that doesn’t feel mysterious to me. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. I mean, I sleep… maybe not always perfectly, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether I’m waking up more than usual or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there for a bit, but I think most people do that, especially when you’ve got things on your mind or you’re getting older. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. At 74, there are days where I’m not exactly springing out of a chair, you know? But that feels more like just normal stiffness or taking my time, not something where I’d say, “What’s wrong with me, why am I moving so slow?”\n\nAnd as far as being restless, unable to sit still… I don’t really think of myself that way. I might shift around or get up if I’m uncomfortable or if something’s on my mind, but I haven’t noticed pacing around or feeling like I can’t stay seated. If anything, I probably sit and think too much sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not something that stands out to me. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things throughout the day, even small or minor issues, not just the bigger stressors like work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a bunch of little things all day long, no. I mean, I worry, sure—everybody worries, and I probably get stuck thinking about certain things more than I’d like sometimes. But when you say “a variety of different things,” like I’m jumping from one small issue to another all day, I don’t know that I recognize that in myself.\n\nIf something’s important or it feels like it needs handling, then yes, it can stay in my head. I might go over it more than once, think about what I should’ve done or what I need to do next. But I don’t usually catch myself worrying about every minor thing, like, I don’t know, a tiny errand or some small inconvenience. At least it doesn’t stand out to me that way.\n\nI guess I have a hard time separating normal thinking from worrying, too. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t necessarily call it worry about lots of different things. So… no, not that I’ve really noticed. Not in that broad, constant kind of way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious most days, even when there isn’t something specific happening that would explain it, like a general background feeling of anxiety that’s always there?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it as a constant background anxiety, no. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. I might be tense or preoccupied and not really call it anxiety, you know? But if you’re asking whether I wake up most days already anxious, or sit there feeling nervous even when nothing is happening, I can’t really say that’s been obvious to me.\n\nI guess I’d put it more like… I have things on my mind at times, and certain situations can get to me, but I haven’t noticed this general, unexplained anxious feeling being there all the time. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I know it might sound like I’m splitting hairs, but when you say “many different things,” I picture someone worrying about one thing, then another, then another, all unrelated, like the car, the bills, the weather, the neighbor, some appointment, and it just keeps jumping around for no clear reason. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. Or if I am, I’m not recognizing it as that.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, but I have trouble saying whether that’s worry or just thinking too much. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are times when I’ll tell myself, “Alright, stop going over this, think about something else,” and my mind may still circle back to whatever it was. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. But if everything seems settled and there isn’t anything I can point to, I don’t really recognize myself as worrying anyway. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or low on energy even on days when you haven’t been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, at 74, I don’t always know what’s just being older and what’s something I’m supposed to pay closer attention to. Some days I’m not exactly energetic, but I wouldn’t automatically say, “Oh, I’m tired even though I didn’t do anything.” It’s hard to separate out.\n\nIf I’ve been moving around or doing things, then being tired makes sense to me. On quieter days, I might feel a little slowed down, maybe not as ready to get going, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I’m wiped out for no reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when you know, logically, that there isn’t really a problem, like when everything seems to be going okay on the surface?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, if everything really seems okay on the surface, I don’t usually catch myself thinking, “There’s a problem,” when I know there isn’t one. But sometimes my mind will still kind of poke around at things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I might think, “Well, maybe I’m missing something,” or I’ll go back over something that happened and wonder if I handled it right. But I’m not sure whether that counts as worrying when there’s no real problem, or whether it’s just me being in my own head too much.\n\nI guess logically, if things are fine, I can usually tell myself they’re fine. I don’t think I’m sitting there inventing trouble out of nowhere. But I can be a little slow to let something go if it feels unsettled, even if nothing is actually wrong at that moment. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly, because I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exact thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling down, sad, or depressed most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that in a clean way. I mean… “down” is one of those words where I’m not always sure what people mean by it. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around cheerful all the time, but I also don’t know that I’d say I’m depressed most days for no reason.\n\nSome days I’m quieter, or I don’t feel like myself, and I may not have much enthusiasm, but I don’t always stop and label it as sadness. And if I do feel low, I usually feel like there’s something connected to it, even if it’s not some big dramatic thing. I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself feeling sad just out of nowhere when everything is fine.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say not clearly. I may have days like that, but I don’t know that it’s most days, and I don’t know that I’d call it depressed exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different areas of your life—like work, family, health, or other responsibilities—even when those worries aren’t directly connected to each other?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it spreads across a lot of different areas like that. I mean, I can worry, sure, but I don’t know that I’m sitting there worrying about work, family, health, responsibilities, all separate things at once.\n\nSometimes one thing will get in my head and I’ll kind of chew on it longer than I probably need to, but it’s usually tied to whatever set it off. I don’t think I’ve noticed it jumping from one category to another, like, “Now I’m worried about this, now that, now something else.” At least not in a way I could clearly point to.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I wouldn’t say yes strongly. It doesn’t feel like many different areas all at the same time, or unrelated worries piling up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you found that you don’t care about or look forward to things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or going to church, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I mean, I’ve been thinking about that kind of thing because people ask, “Are you still doing the things you like?” and I don’t always know how to measure it.\n\nThere are times when I don’t feel as eager to start something, maybe I’ll put it off or I’ll think, “Eh, maybe later,” but I don’t know if that means I don’t care about it anymore. Sometimes once I’m actually there or doing the thing, it’s not like I hate it or feel nothing. It’s more like getting myself to it can feel harder, if that makes sense.\n\nWith family or normal routines, I still know they matter to me. I wouldn’t say I stopped caring. Looking forward to things… that’s a little harder. I don’t know that I look forward to things in the same clear way all the time, but I also can’t point to something and say, “I used to enjoy that and now I just don’t.” I haven’t really noticed it in a definite way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more that I may be less enthusiastic sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been days where you truly didn’t care about anything or couldn’t feel any pleasure, even in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, family, or church?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say I’ve had days where I truly didn’t care about anything. That sounds… stronger than what I’ve noticed in myself.\n\nI mean, there are definitely days where I’m kind of flat, or where I don’t feel much spark about doing things. Like something I normally might enjoy, I might look at it and think, “I don’t know, not right now,” or it takes more effort to get into it. But once I’m with family or doing something familiar, I don’t think it’s like I can’t feel any pleasure at all. It’s not completely gone.\n\nChurch, family, little routines… I still know those things matter. I may not always feel excited beforehand, but I wouldn’t say I don’t care. And “couldn’t feel any pleasure” feels like a pretty absolute way to put it. I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not in a way I’d recognize and say, yes, that was a whole day where nothing meant anything.\n\nSo… no, not really. Maybe less enthusiasm sometimes, but not truly no caring or no pleasure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think… mostly when I’ve felt low, it’s been tied to what’s been going on, if I’m being honest. It’s hard for me to separate it out from the warehouse situation.

I came in because ever since they changed my hours and put in that new inventory system, I feel like I’m just not keeping up the way I used to. And it’s not just that I’m irritated at work or having a bad day there. It’s followed me home. I’ve snapped at my family over little things, and afterward I sit there thinking, “Why did I talk like that?”

So if you’re asking whether I feel down out of nowhere, when nothing stressful is happening… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. The low feeling seems connected to all this. Some mornings I’ll be sitting there with my work boots on, and I just kind of freeze, wondering if I can even face going back into that place again. As far as feeling drained when I haven’t done much… I can’t say I’ve really noticed that in a clear way. Maybe there are times I sit down and don’t feel like getting back up right away, but I don’t know if that’s low energy or just being tired from the day, or just being older.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not in a way that stands out to me. I haven’t really been thinking, “Why am I so exhausted when I didn’t do anything?” It’s more that when I’m tired, I can usually explain it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately—like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have a clear answer to that. And sometimes I might wake up and look at the clock, but I can’t say it’s been this definite pattern where I’m up every night or waking way too early and can’t get back to sleep.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a big sleep problem lately. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me enough that I could describe it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're feeling stressed or down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, moving slower—maybe sometimes I take longer to get going, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt like I’m physically slowed down in some unusual way. I mean, I do get worried when there’s something in front of me, something I’m thinking about or something that feels unsettled, but I’m not sure I walk around most days with anxiety just humming there for no reason.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to judge, because sometimes you don’t notice your own state until somebody asks you directly. If it is there, I suppose I haven’t been labeling it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worried about many different things in your life, even when those things aren’t necessarily connected or when there isn’t a clear reason for the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d put it that way, no. I don’t know that I see myself doing that.\n\nI can get caught up in something if it feels important or unresolved, and then I may turn it over in my head more than I need to. But it’s usually tied to something, at least in my mind. It’s not like I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m anxious about ten different unrelated things for no reason. At my age, you’ve got enough history in your head that thoughts come up, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally worried about everything in life. So… no, not clearly. I haven’t really noticed that kind of scattered, across-the-board worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worrying, even when you try to focus on something else or tell yourself not to think about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as being unable to control it in the way you might mean.\n\nSometimes if something feels unfinished or like I haven’t figured it out, it has a way of popping back up. I might try to watch TV or do something around the house, and then I realize I’m thinking about it again. But other times I can distract myself, or it fades if I’m busy enough. So it’s not like I’m always fighting with my thoughts every minute.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I do get stuck on things sometimes, but whether that’s “hard to control worrying” or just me being preoccupied, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say yes, I can’t control my worry, because I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific that you need to be concerned about, like when things are actually going fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. If things are actually going fine, I’m not sure I’d say I’m sitting there worrying just to worry.\n\nNow, sometimes I may still be thinking about something, because my mind doesn’t always just go quiet, but I don’t know that I’d call that worry when there’s nothing specific attached to it. I might be sitting there and have a thought come through, or sort of review things in my head, but it’s not like I’m feeling alarmed about nothing in particular.\n\nI guess what I’m trying to say is, when I do worry, it usually feels connected to something, even if maybe someone else wouldn’t think it’s a big enough reason. I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… I don’t know, not clearly. I can’t say I’ve noticed that in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when you know, logically, that there isn’t really a problem, like when everything seems to be going okay on the surface?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean… “down” is one of those words where I’m not always sure what people mean by it. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around cheerful all the time, but I also don’t know that I’d say I’m depressed most days for no reason.\n\nSome days I’m quieter, or I don’t feel like myself, and I may not have much enthusiasm, but I don’t always stop and label it as sadness. I’ve missed shifts, I’ve missed things I said I’d do at church, and then I feel worse because that’s not how I’ve usually been. I’ve snapped at my family over little things, and afterward I sit there thinking, “Why did I talk like that?”\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I feel down out of nowhere, when nothing stressful is happening… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. The low feeling seems connected to all this. Some mornings I’ll be sitting there with my work boots on, and I just kind of freeze, wondering if I can even face going back into that place again. It feels like everything started coming apart after those changes at work."
  }
]
